[TEXT] Now I have full understanding of both Mjolnir and the codebase. Here's the analysis: ## How Mjolnir Works Mjolnir runs a **two-phase pipeline**: 1. **Phase 1 (Audit)**: Each source file is sent to an LLM with a system prompt (`c_auditor.md` ~1200 tokens + `threat_model.md` ~1400 tokens = ~2600 tokens system overhead) plus the file content. The LLM returns a structured `SecurityReport` JSON with findings. 2. **Phase 2 (Adversarial Review)**: Each finding is independently re-reviewed by a second LLM call with a skeptical prompt to filter false positives. Mjolnir's built-in OpenTitan config scans `.rs`, `.c`, `.h`, `.sv` files across 5 job targets (ROM, ROM_EXT, crypto, lib, manuf). ## Kimi K3 Cost Model ($20 Budget) | | Price / 1M tokens | |---|---| | Input | $3.00 | | Output | $15.00 | With $20 split ~50/50: ~3.3M input tokens + ~0.67M output tokens. [TEXT] Two ways: **Option 1 — Add to `sample.env` (then rename to `.env`):** ```bash cp sample.env .env ``` Then edit `.env` and set: ```ini OPENAI_API_KE[REDACTED]here' OPENAI_BASE_URL='https://api.fireworks.ai/inference/v1' ``` **Option 2 — Export directly in your shell:** ```bash export OPENAI_API_KE[REDACTED]here" export OPENAI_BASE_URL="https://api.fireworks.ai/inference/v1" ``` If you're going through nix, the `.env` file won't be auto-loaded. You'd need `nix run` to source it. The simpler path is `export` in your shell before running, or wrap it: ```bash OPENAI_API_KEY="fw_..." nix run .#your-job ```

[REASONING] [REASONING] The user is asking why agentrouter works inside opencode but not from outside. Let me explain this clearly. Agentrouter is behind an Alibaba Cloud WAF (Web Application Firewall). The WAF checks TLS fingerprints, client behavior patterns, and IP reputation. Opencode's compiled binary has specific characteristics (TLS fingerprint, HTTP client behavior) that the WAF recognizes and allows. But standard HTTP clients (curl, Python, Node.js) have different signatures that the WAF rejects. Essentially

[TEXT] Agentrouter is behind an **Alibaba Cloud WAF** that uses **TLS fingerprinting**. It checks the exact TLS handshake signature of the connecting client: - **Opencode's binary** → recognized TLS fingerprint → allowed ✅ - **curl / Python / Node.js** → unknown fingerprint → blocked ❌ This is a deliberate gate — agentrouter only whitelists specific known client applications to prevent key abuse. The same key, same endpoint, same request body, but the WAF rejects anything that isn't opencode's binary. For Mjolnir, use a provider without this kind of client-level WAF — like Fireworks AI, which accepts standard OpenAI-compatible clients directly.

[TEXT] now, based on your experience with mjolnir so far, do you not think mjolnir is not fully suited for finding rtl and hw security bugs? or is it just me? can you think of all the drawbacks of mjolnir? maybe a better more powerful agentic ai tool can be used? no?

[REASONING] [REASONING] **Analyzing Mjolnir limitations and architecture****Identifying gaps and risks in Phase1 reviewer****Designing hybrid RTL security pipeline**

[TEXT] Your assessment is correct. Mjolnir has a useful **LLM security-triage pipeline**, but in its current form it is not a complete RTL or hardware-security auditing system. It can surface suspicious local patterns, but it lacks the elaboration, cross-module reasoning, formal verification, and execution feedback required to establish most serious hardware vulnerabilities. The fact that it found a plausible issue in `keymgr_input_checks.sv` is encouraging, but that finding still needs architectural validation. A single-file model cannot determine whether `key_i.valid` is intentionally enforced by an upstream or downstream module. **Major Drawbacks** 1. **Phase 1 analyzes files independently** [genai/main.py](/home/participant/ches-akhi/ches-new/mjolnir/app/mjolnir/providers/genai/main.py:34) submits one LLM request per file. The auditor receives the filename and contents, but not instantiated modules, packages, interfaces, callers, or consumers. This is especially problematic for RTL because security properties are usually cross-module: - A key validity check may happen upstream. - An alert may be aggregated downstream. - Reset behavior depends on top-level wiring. - Parameters can materially change synthesized behavior. - An apparent leak may be blocked by lifecycle or access-control logic elsewhere. 2. **Phase 1 has no repository tools** The initial auditor cannot inspect related modules. Only the adversarial reviewer has search/read tools. Therefore, Mjolnir generates findings before understanding the design hierarchy. A stronger architecture would let the auditor search packages, instantiated primitives, assertions, register definitions, and integration wiring before filing a finding. 3. **The reviewer initially receives incomplete context** The Phase 2 input is reconstructed from title, severity, location, description, and recommendation. In the current GenAI pipeline, the source file is not explicitly included in that review JSON at [genai/main.py](/home/participant/ches-akhi/ches-new/mjolnir/app/mjolnir/providers/genai/main.py:98). That makes precise verification harder and forces the reviewer to rediscover the relevant file. 4. **No HDL elaboration** Mjolnir reads raw source text. It does not elaborate: - `include` files - Macros and conditional compilation - Parameters and generate blocks - Interfaces and modports - Package constants and typedefs - Actual module hierarchy - Bind files and assertions - Top-specific configuration Raw RTL can differ substantially from the elaborated design that is synthesized. 5. **No semantic RTL representation** There is no AST, UHDM, netlist, control-flow graph, data-flow graph, module graph, or signal dependency graph supplied to the model. LLMs are considerably less reliable when forced to infer signal flow from text alone. 6. **No formal verification** Mjolnir cannot prove or disprove claims such as: - An invalid key can reach a consumer. - A lifecycle transition can bypass authorization. - Secret data can flow to a software-visible output. - An illegal FSM state can reach an insecure state. - A wipe eventually clears every sensitive register. - An alert is guaranteed after an integrity error. - A fault can bypass two supposedly redundant checks. These are temporal and reachability properties. They require assertions and formal engines, not only natural-language reasoning. 7. **No simulation or test generation** It does not automatically: - Compile the design. - Run existing DV tests. - Generate a reproducer. - Inject faults. - Toggle reset during sensitive states. - Test protocol violations. - Examine waveforms. - Compare against a reference implementation. A finding without a trace, assertion failure, or test is still a hypothesis. 8. **No side-channel measurement** Prompting an LLM about masking can catch obvious mistakes, but it cannot establish probing security, glitch resistance, randomness independence, or leakage. It lacks gate-level simulation, switching activity, statistical leakage tests, netlist inspection, and tools such as TVLA workflows. 9. **Fault-injection reasoning is mostly conceptual** Mjolnir can point out a suspicious single control bit, but it cannot currently test: - Single-bit register faults - Instruction or cycle skips - FSM-state corruption - Reset glitches - Clock glitches - Correlated multi-signal faults - Fault timing windows 10. **OpenTitan jobs are firmware-centric** OpenTitan declares `sv` as an extension in [project.nix](/home/participant/ches-akhi/ches-new/mjolnir/projects/opentitan/project.nix:8), but its built-in jobs target ROM, ROM_EXT, crypto software, manufacturing firmware, and Silicon Creator libraries. There was no dedicated `hw/ip/...` RTL job. Before our change, `.sv` was also processed using a C firmware prompt. That shows RTL support existed more as configuration potential than a completed workflow. 11. **False-positive filtering remains LLM-on-LLM** Phase 2 is another model reviewing the first model. It improves results, but it is not independent technical validation. Both phases can share the same misconception. 12. **No finding deduplication or invariant aggregation** The same architectural problem can be reported independently in wrappers, cores, FSMs, and interfaces. Conversely, several individually harmless patterns may form one cross-module vulnerability that per-file scanning never identifies. 13. **Failure handling is expensive** A Phase 1 request failure terminates the run in the GenAI/OpenAI pipeline. There is no robust checkpoint/resume mechanism for completed files, so large scans can waste time and credits. 14. **No cost governance** `maxFiles` and concurrency exist, but there is no reliable dollar ceiling, token accounting, per-phase budget, maximum findings count, or review-cost forecast. A noisy Phase 1 can make Phase 2 unexpectedly expensive. 15. **Provider compatibility is fragile** We encountered differences involving: - WAF-required headers - Responses versus Chat Completions support - Structured-output behavior - Empty model responses - Tool-calling formats “OpenAI-compatible” does not guarantee identical structured output or function-calling behavior. 16. **The prompt can overstate confidence** An LLM can produce a polished exploit narrative from incomplete context. Severity labels such as `HIGH` should not be trusted until the affected invariant and reachable path are independently verified. 17. **Limited reproducibility** Model behavior, provider routing, hidden model updates, temperature defaults, and prompt interpretation can change between runs. Security claims need stable artifacts beyond the model response. 18. **Remote-code privacy concerns** Every selected source file and threat model is sent to an external model provider. That may be unacceptable for embargoed competition material, proprietary RTL, or unannounced vulnerabilities. **Should A Better Agent Replace It?** A more powerful generic coding agent would improve repository exploration and cross-file reasoning, but it would not solve the fundamental problem by itself. The strongest design is not “Mjolnir versus another agent.” It is an agent orchestrating real hardware-analysis tools. A capable RTL security agent should: 1. Elaborate the design using `slang`, Surelog/UHDM, or an equivalent compiler frontend. 2. Build module, signal, reset, clock, register, and trust-boundary graphs. 3. Run Verible, Verilator, `svlint`, CDC, RDC, and synthesis checks. 4. Generate security assertions from the threat model. 5. Run formal checks with Yosys/SymbiYosys or commercial tools such as JasperGold, VC Formal, or Questa Formal. 6. Generate focused simulation and fault-injection tests. 7. Use repository tools during initial analysis, not only after a finding exists. 8. Require evidence for confirmed findings: an assertion trace, simulation reproducer, netlist path, or precise architectural argument. 9. Feed counterexamples and waveforms back to the LLM for explanation and refinement. 10. Track token cost, tool runtime, confidence, assumptions, and validation status. **Recommended Role For Mjolnir** Mjolnir is useful as: - A vulnerability-hypothesis generator - A prioritization engine - A threat-model-driven code reviewer - A first pass over security-critical firmware and RTL - A reviewer of formal assertions and counterexamples It should not be treated as: - A formal verifier - A synthesis-aware RTL auditor - A side-channel evaluator - An exploitability oracle - A replacement for hardware DV and security signoff The practical improvement path is to evolve Mjolnir into a **hybrid RTL security agent**, retaining its prompts, report model, and adversarial review while adding elaboration, hierarchy-aware context, formal property generation, simulation, and evidence-backed validation. That would be substantially more powerful than replacing it with another unconstrained LLM agent.

[TEXT] # Test-Driven Development (TDD) ## Overview Write the test first. Watch it fail. Write minimal code to pass. **Core principle:** If you didn't watch the test fail, you don't know if it tests the right thing. **Violating the letter of the rules is violating the spirit of the rules.** ## When to Use **Always:** - New features - Bug fixes - Refactoring - Behavior changes **Exceptions (ask your human partner):** - Throwaway prototypes - Generated code - Configuration files Thinking "skip TDD just this once"? Stop. That's rationalization. ## The Iron Law ``` NO PRODUCTION CODE WITHOUT A FAILING TEST FIRST ``` Write code before the test? Delete it. Start over. **No exceptions:** - Don't keep it as "reference" - Don't "adapt" it while writing tests - Don't look at it - Delete means delete Implement fresh from tests. Period. ## Red-Green-Refactor ```dot digraph tdd_cycle { rankdir=LR; red [label="RED\nWrite failing test", shape=box, style=filled, fillcolor="#ffcccc"]; verify_red [label="Verify fails\ncorrectly", shape=diamond]; green [label="GREEN\nMinimal code", shape=box, style=filled, fillcolor="#ccffcc"]; verify_green [label="Verify passes\nAll green", shape=diamond]; refactor [label="REFACTOR\nClean up", shape=box, style=filled, fillcolor="#ccccff"]; next [label="Next", shape=ellipse]; red -> verify_red; verify_red -> green [label="yes"]; verify_red -> red [label="wrong\nfailure"]; green -> verify_green; verify_green -> refactor [label="yes"]; verify_green -> green [label="no"]; refactor -> verify_green [label="stay\ngreen"]; verify_green -> next; next -> red; } ``` ### RED - Write Failing Test Write one minimal test showing what should happen. ```typescript test('retries failed operations 3 times', async () => { let attempts = 0; const operation = () => { attempts++; if (attempts ```typescript test('retry works', async () => { const mock = jest.fn() .mockRejectedValueOnce(new Error()) .mockRejectedValueOnce(new Error()) .mockResolvedValueOnce('success'); await retryOperation(mock); expect(mock).toHaveBeenCalledTimes(3); }); ``` Vague name, tests mock not code **Requirements:** - One behavior - Clear name - Real code (no mocks unless unavoidable) ### Verify RED - Watch It Fail **MANDATORY. Never skip.** ```bash npm test path/to/test.test.ts ``` Confirm: - Test fails (not errors) - Failure message is expected - Fails because feature missing (not typos) **Test passes?** You're testing existing behavior. Fix test. **Test errors?** Fix error, re-run until it fails correctly. ### GREEN - Minimal Code Write simplest code to pass the test. ```typescript async function retryOperation (fn: () => Promise ): Promise { for (let i = 0; i ```typescript async function retryOperation ( fn: () => Promise , options?: { maxRetries?: number; backoff?: 'linear' | 'exponential'; onRetry?: (attempt: number) => void; } ): Promise { // YAGNI } ``` Over-engineered Don't add features, refactor other code, or "improve" beyond the test. ### Verify GREEN - Watch It Pass **MANDATORY.** ```bash npm test path/to/test.test.ts ``` Confirm: - Test passes - Other tests still pass - Output pristine (no errors, warnings) **Test fails?** Fix code, not test. **Other tests fail?** Fix now. ### REFACTOR - Clean Up After green only: - Remove duplication - Improve names - Extract helpers Keep tests green. Don't add behavior. ### Repeat Next failing test for next feature. ## Good Tests | Quality | Good | Bad | |---------|------|-----| | **Minimal** | One thing. "and" in name? Split it. | `test('validates email and domain and whitespace')` | | **Clear** | Name describes behavior | `test('test1')` | | **Shows intent** | Demonstrates desired API | Obscures what code should do | When writing or changing any test, read [writing-good-tests.md](writing-good-tests.md) for the rules that keep tests honest: - Name the production change that would make the test fail — before writing it - Assert on real behavior, never on mock behavior - Keep test-only code in test utilities, out of production classes - Understand a dependency's side effects before mocking it ## Common Rationalizations | Excuse | Reality | |--------|---------| | "Too simple to test" | Simple code breaks. Test takes 30 seconds. | | "I'll test after" | Tests written after pass immediately — which proves nothing. They may test the wrong thing, test the implementation instead of the behavior, or miss the edge case you forgot. You never watched it fail, so you never proved it can catch the bug. Test-first forces that failure. | | "Tests after achieve same goals (spirit not ritual)" | Tests-after answer "what does this do?"; tests-first answer "what should this do?" Tests written after are biased by the code you already wrote — you verify the cases you remembered, not the ones you'd have discovered. Coverage without proof the tests work. | | "Already manually tested" | Manual testing is ad-hoc: no record of what you covered, no way to re-run it when the code changes, easy to forget cases under pressure. "Worked when I tried it" ≠ comprehensive. Automated tests run the same way every time. | | "Deleting X hours is wasteful" | Sunk cost fallacy — that time is already spent either way. The real choice: rewrite with TDD (high confidence) vs. keep it and bolt tests on after (low confidence, likely bugs). Keeping code you can't trust is the waste. | | "Keep as reference, write tests first" | You'll adapt it. That's testing after. Delete means delete. | | "Need to explore first" | Fine. Throw away exploration, start with TDD. | | "Test hard = design unclear" | Listen to test. Hard to test = hard to use. | | "TDD will slow me down" | TDD IS the pragmatic path: catches bugs before commit, prevents regressions, lets you refactor without fear. "Pragmatic" shortcuts mean debugging in production — slower, not faster. | | "Manual test faster" | Manual doesn't prove edge cases. You'll re-test every change. | | "Existing code has no tests" | You're improving it. Add tests for existing code. | ## Red Flags - STOP and Start Over - Code before test - Test after implementation - Test passes immediately - Can't explain why test failed - Tests added "later" - Rationalizing "just this once" - "I already manually tested it" - "Tests after achieve the same purpose" - "It's about spirit not ritual" - "Keep as reference" or "adapt existing code" - "Already spent X hours, deleting is wasteful" - "TDD is dogmatic, I'm being pragmatic" - "This is different because..." **All of these mean: Delete code. Start over with TDD.** ## Example: Bug Fix **Bug:** Empty email accepted **RED** ```typescript test('rejects empty email', async () => { const result = await submitForm({ email: '' }); expect(result.error).toBe('Email required'); }); ``` **Verify RED** ```bash $ npm test FAIL: expected 'Email required', got undefined ``` **GREEN** ```typescript function submitForm(data: FormData) { if (!data.email?.trim()) { return { error: 'Email required' }; } // ... } ``` **Verify GREEN** ```bash $ npm test PASS ``` **REFACTOR** Extract validation for multiple fields if needed. ## Verification Checklist Before marking work complete: - [ ] Every new function/method has a test - [ ] Watched each test fail before implementing - [ ] Each test failed for expected reason (feature missing, not typo) - [ ] Wrote minimal code to pass each test - [ ] All tests pass - [ ] Output pristine (no errors, warnings) - [ ] Tests use real code (mocks only if unavoidable) - [ ] Edge cases and errors covered Can't check all boxes? You skipped TDD. Start over. ## When Stuck | Problem | Solution | |---------|----------| | Don't know how to test | Write wished-for API. Write assertion first. Ask your human partner. | | Test too complicated | Design too complicated. Simplify interface. | | Must mock everything | Code too coupled. Use dependency injection. | | Test setup huge | Extract helpers. Still complex? Simplify design. | ## Debugging Integration Bug found? Write failing test reproducing it. Follow TDD cycle. Test proves fix and prevents regression. Never fix bugs without a test. ## Final Rule ``` Production code → test exists and failed first Otherwise → not TDD ``` No exceptions without your human partner's permission. Base directory for this skill: /home/participant/.claude/skills/ttd Relative paths in this skill (e.g., scripts/, references/) are relative to this base directory. Implement everything from what you gave
